# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-24

## Diffusion Language Model
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[What Makes a Good Unmasking Policy? A Controlled Study of Learned Unmasking in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31576v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Masked diffusion language models generate tokens in parallel, but their efficiency depends on which predictions are committed. Learned unmasking policies improve the speed--accuracy trade-off, yet differ in supervision, inputs, and capacity, obscuring which choices matter. We view supervised policies as distilling a decoding procedure through a teacher and examine independently what the teacher labels as committable, on which states the policy imitates it, what the policy observes, and how much the number of function evaluations (NFE) must decrease to offset policy overhead. We find that labeling predictions matching the final output is overly permissive at low NFE, and hidden states are weak alone but can complement confidence. About a 10% NFE reduction suffices to offset policy overhead for policies up to 7.5M parameters across tested settings. We therefore train a policy on trajectory-contraction targets, which accept only commitments staying on a reference trajectory, using confidence and hidden states. On LLaDA-8B-Instruct, the resulting 856K-parameter policy achieves the best accuracy--NFE frontier and highest throughput on GSM8K, is competitive at moderate targets on MATH-500 and HumanEval, and largely transfers to Dream-7B-Instruct.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint.32 pages, 11 figures</p></details> |
| **[Diffusion Language Models for Perplexity-Aware Black-Box Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31558v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>\emph{Continuous-space} attacks find prompts that trigger malicious behaviors, such as overthinking, in black-box LLMs by sampling or optimizing over a low-dimensional Gaussian latent space and decoding it to text through a \emph{random mapping} and nearest-token lookup. Because the random mapping is not learned from language, its prompts can be highly unnatural, and as such easily detectable through input guardrails, and the risk estimates they yield are not grounded in natural prompts. In this paper, we demonstrate that the same continuous latent-space framework can be coupled directly to a learned generative distribution over language, a continuous diffusion language model (dLLM), which removes the artificial random mapping while preserving the standard-Gaussian latent formulation and the downstream sampling or optimization procedure. We evaluate this approach under two settings: sampling-based estimation using \emph{RareTrap} and optimization-based search using \emph{ThinkTrap}. We find that character-level perplexity drops from $10^3$--$10^7$ to 3--5 in RareTrap and from 20--28 to 4.3--5.4 in ThinkTrap. We also find that model risk estimates can differ by an order of magnitude across the two mappings.</p></details> |  |
| **[Harmful Responses Are Jailbreak Templates: Transferring Autoregressive Jailbreaks to Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31331v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) enable flexible masked generation through bidirectional context modeling, but this capability also introduces a distinct jailbreak attack surface. We find that a harmful response to a given query naturally serves as an effective jailbreak template: simply masking a subset of its tokens can induce a dLLM to reconstruct harmful content. Based on this observation, we propose RMT-Jailbreak, a general framework that transfers existing autoregressive (AR) jailbreak attacks to dLLMs through their induced harmful responses. RMT-Jailbreak converts an AR-generated harmful response into a high-mask-ratio template using uniform token retention, and further applies prompt-based safety filtering to mask any remaining visible harmful content. The refined template is then provided to the target dLLM for bidirectional reconstruction. Extensive experiments across multiple AR jailbreak methods and target dLLMs demonstrate the effectiveness and generality of RMT-Jailbreak, revealing strong cross-paradigm transferability of jailbreak attacks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Trion-1: Unifying Language, Protein Sequence, and Structure with Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31215v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We envision a general-purpose protein foundation model as a unified, interactive model of sequence, structure, and function, capable of understanding, generation, and cross-modal translation while retaining the instruction-following and reasoning capabilities of modern LLMs. Toward this vision, we introduce Trion-1, the first native, unified, multimodal diffusion language model (dLLM) for proteins. Trion-1 is built on two key design choices: (1) a unified discrete token space that jointly represents sequence, structure, and natural language; (2) a dLLM backbone that replaces strict left-to-right factorization with bidirectional or semi-bidirectional token interactions, enabling global residue communication and long-range contact modeling that are naturally suited to structure modeling. This formulation unifies six core capabilities: folding, inverse folding, sequence understanding, structure understanding, sequence design, and structure design, while retaining the native language capabilities of general-purpose LLMs. On established benchmarks, Trion-1 achieves broadly competitive performance. In folding, Trion-1 achieves a TM-score above 0.8 with a single sample under strict homology control, matching or exceeding the existing tri-modal model using 200 samples. For sequence understanding, Trion-1 performs on par with top-tier LLMs such as Claude Opus 4.7 (62.37%). These results support well-aligned sequence-structure-language representations and strong cross-modal generative capabilities.</p></details> |  |
| **[Different Tasks, Different Timesteps: Temporal Task-Aware Quantization for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31198v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) introduce unique quantization challenges due to their iterative denoising process. In this work, we reveal that quantization sensitivity is both taskand time-dependent, with different downstream tasks exhibiting distinct precision requirements across denoising steps. We further show that these patterns can be predicted without groundtruth labels or task-specific evaluation metrics. Building on these findings, we introduce T 2Quant, a task and time-aware mixedprecision quantization framework that uses a label-free sensitivity signal to optimally allocate activation precision across denoising steps under a fixed bit-width budget. Extensive experiments across three DLMs and six benchmarks demonstrate that T 2Quant consistently improves the average accuracy of existing quantization methods, achieving gains of up to 6.7 percentage points under the same budget.</p></details> | preprint |
| **[SetCommit: Coordinated Token Commitments for Few-Step Diffusion Language Model Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31120v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models generate multiple token positions in parallel, but few-step decoding makes parallel commitment difficult: tokens that appear reliable individually may be jointly inconsistent, while premature commitments can prevent later context-dependent refinement. In this paper, we introduce SetCommit, a training-free method for coordinated parallel token commitment. Instead of selecting tokens independently by confidence, SetCommit selects a commitment set that balances token reliability against local joint commitment risk, preserving conditioning opportunities for subsequent denoising steps. It further performs budget-neutral local exchanges, replacing vulnerable earlier commitments when newly revealed context supports more reliable alternatives. SetCommit requires no retraining or architectural modification and is compatible with existing diffusion decoding strategies. Experiments with LLaDA-8B and Dream-7B on GSM8K, MATH-500, MBPP, and HumanEval show accuracy/pass@1 gains of up to 7.4 percentage points over native decoding at 32 model evaluations. At common quality targets, geometric-mean incremental speedups range from $1.44$ to $1.74\times$. Code is available at https://github.com/Kissmetothemoon/SetCommit.</p></details> |  |
| **[Hacking Generative Perplexity: Why Unconditional Text Evaluation Needs New Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08417v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models (DLMs) have emerged as the leading non-autoregressive alternative to language modeling. Because their likelihoods are intractable, generative perplexity (gen-PPL) has become the de facto standard for reporting unconditional generation quality. We analyze why gen-PPL is unreliable as a measure of generation quality. Across samples, it is minimized by always emitting the scorer's most likely sequence. Per-sample unigram entropy does not measure this loss of diversity. Within a sample, gen-PPL rewards predictability under the scorer, which need not correspond to text quality. To make this failure explicit, we construct four training-free samplers that reach or beat trained-model gen-PPL on LM1B and OpenWebText at non-degenerate unigram entropy, while producing text that is incoherent by construction. We then rebenchmark recent DLMs using distributional discrepancies and diversity diagnostics, and compare their rankings with LLM judgments. As part of this evaluation, we introduce a compression-based discrepancy that measures local coverage and fidelity, the two directions of the KL divergence between real and generated text. We argue that DLM progress should be evaluated with a set of metrics that expose different properties of generated text.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prese...</summary><p>Presented at the ICML 2026 Workshop on Structured Probabilistic Inference & Generative Modeling (SPIGM), Seoul, South Korea</p></details> |
| **[DConstrain: Compiling Context-Parameterized Transitions for Diffusion Language Model Constrained Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30920v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models (DLMs) decode multiple tokens in parallel, so individually valid tokens can be incompatible together under a schema. Exact joint inference over a grammar graph enforces compatibility, but it requires token-labeled successors across many hypothetical parser configurations. Encoding stacks and field histories in compiled states can cause exponential growth, which forces prior exact decoders to fix property orders and exclude valid outputs. We present DConstrain, which compiles schemas into context-parameterized transition programs. Cold compilation specializes token consumption to local grammar states while retaining context-dependent stack operations, environment guards, and caller-dependent deferred parsing symbolically. Tracking field histories as runtime values supports arbitrary property order without enumerating field subsets offline. At runtime, warm compilation binds reached configurations to these programs to construct exact successors, overlapping with the model forward pass because graph construction requires no model scores. Furthermore, token-effect grouping merges tokens with identical compiled effects before runtime contexts are bound, so groups are reused across stacks and environments even when concrete successors differ. Objective-specific score aggregation and token restoration provably preserve both the Viterbi optimum and the token-labeled path distribution of ungrouped inference. Across three benchmarks and two DLM families, DConstrain compiles 100% of BFCL schemas under arbitrary property orders, whereas an optimized finite-state baseline compiles only 71.30-85.81%. Under arbitrary property order, it reduces BFCL end-to-end decoding latency by 6.65-46.26 times with 100% schema validity and raises DiffusionGemma's BFCL accuracy from 37.27% under fixed order to 81.56%.</p></details> | <details><summary>39 pa...</summary><p>39 pages, 6 figures, 12 tables</p></details> |
| **[Loopholing All the Way: Language Modeling with pure Self-Conditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30068v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) use Self-Conditioning (SC), or Loopholing, to improve their performance. While SC is traditionally seen as an auxiliary heuristic, its empirical success raises the question: can a continuous generative model be based purely on Self-Conditioning? We answer affirmatively with LAW (``Loopholing All the Way''), which propagates the Expected Embedding under the denoiser's prediction across steps, decoding the tokens once, at the end. On Sudoku, LAW outperforms previous Discrete and Continuous Diffusion Models, including Loopholing Discrete Diffusion. On Maze, LAW is the most accurate from 2 to 64 NFE. After training on TinyGSM and sampling at temperature 1 with 1024 NFE, LAW solves 42.2% of GSM8K problems against 34.5% for uniform Loopholing Discrete Diffusion. Additionally, fine-tuning a pre-trained Uniform Diffusion Model (Duo) with LAW increases its accuracy from 17.2% to 48.1%. Finally, on OpenWebText, LAW pushes the Pareto frontier and approaches Autoregressive Models (ARMs) with 64 NFE. Find our code at https://github.com/ilievanadezhda/law.</p></details> |  |
| **[Looped Uniform Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30066v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Uniform diffusion language models (UDLMs) generate text by iteratively denoising sequences of ordinary vocabulary tokens, any of which may be corrupted and subsequently revised. During training, UDLMs must denoise from a context that may itself be unreliable, whereas masked diffusion language models (MDLMs) always condition on a clean context. This challenging training objective makes UDLMs more FLOP-intensive to train than MDLMs, but it yields stronger downstream reasoning performance. Recently, selective looping, which recursively applies a few early Transformer layers within each denoising step, has been shown to improve training efficiency in MDLMs under an iso-FLOP budget. We investigate looping in UDLMs while keeping the training objective and sampler unchanged. We find that looping significantly improves training efficiency: looped UDLMs reach the validation NLL of a non-looped UDLM trained on about 0.5 trillion tokens with up to 2.8x fewer training FLOPs on OpenWebText and 3.3x fewer on FineWeb-Edu. We also find that, at a matched NFE budget, looped UDLMs reach up to 9.8% lower generation perplexity than the non-looped UDLM, with most gains achieved at moderate loop counts. Finally, we study different loop-count distributions and find that sampling smaller loop counts more frequently during training improves inference efficiency: on downstream tasks, this strategy uses 12% fewer loops than uniform loop-count sampling without degrading accuracy.</p></details> | 27 pages |
| **[Language Modeling with Hyperspherical Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11125v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Discrete Diffusion Language Models progressed rapidly as an alternative to autoregressive (AR) models, motivated by their parallel generation abilities. However, for tractability, discrete diffusion models sample from a factorized distribution, which is less expressive than AR. Recent Flow Language Models (FLMs) apply continuous flows to language, transporting noise to data with a deterministic ODE that avoids factorized sampling. FLMs operate on one-hot vectors whose dimension scales with the vocabulary size, making FLMs costly to train. Moreover, since all distinct one-hot embeddings are equidistant in $\ell_2$, adding Gaussian noise does not have a clear semantic interpretation (unlike images, where Gaussian noise progressively degrades structure). We introduce $\mathbb{S}$-FLM, a latent FLM in the hypersphere. $\mathbb{S}$-FLM generates sequences by rotating vectors in $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$ along a velocity field learned with cross-entropy, avoiding the overhead of materializing one-hot vectors. Previous FLMs match AR in Generative Perplexity (Gen.\ PPL), but samples with high likelihood are not necessarily correct in verifiable domains such as math and code. $\mathbb{S}$-FLM substantially improves continuous flow language models on large-vocabulary reasoning and closes the gap to masked diffusion under standard-temperature sampling ($T=1$), while a gap remains under optimized low-temperature ($T=0.1$) decoding.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Diagnostic Study of Faithfulness in Diffusion Language Models for Table-to-Text Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29602v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models generate by iteratively denoising a masked sequence under bidirectional attention, without the strict left-to-right commitment of autoregressive decoding. Whether this difference affects how reliably they remain grounded in structured inputs is largely unexplored. We study this question in table-to-text generation using ToTTo, where highlighted source cells make factual grounding directly testable. We compare the masked diffusion models MDLM and LLaDA with GPT-2 and GPT-Neo under an identical retrieval-based few-shot protocol, together with a QLoRA-adapted LLaDA, and we score reference similarity and source grounding as separate axes. The two pull apart. Within LLaDA, additional demonstrations raise BLEU from 11.3 to 22.0 while NLI entailment against the source table falls from 0.78 to 0.52; QLoRA reaches the best BLEU of the study, 24.15, and grounds no better for it. Manual annotation shows the failure modes differ in kind: the autoregressive baselines tend to wander off the table, while LLaDA-based models more often stay on topic and swap out individual entities. Reference-based metrics alone are insufficient to assess faithfulness in table-to-text generation.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, including references and appendix. Diagnostic study on source faithfulness in table-to-text generation</p></details> |
| **[A Theoretical Analysis of Why Masked Diffusion Models Mitigate the Reversal Curse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02133v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autoregressive language models (ARMs) suffer from the reversal curse: after learning ''$A$ is $B$,'' they often fail on the reverse query ''$B$ is $A$.'' Masked diffusion language models (MDMs) exhibit this failure in a much weaker form, but the underlying reason has remained unclear. A common explanation attributes this mitigation to their any-order masked training objective. However, observing ''$[\mathbf{M}]$ is $B$'' during training teaches recovery of $A$ from $B$ in one positional configuration, and does not by itself explain why the learned evidence should transfer to the reverse prompt ''$B$ is $[\mathbf{M}]$.'' We provide a theoretical analysis showing that this transfer arises from a parameter-level coupling between forward and reverse positional conditionals: shared Transformer parameters store token-pair evidence, while relative positional encodings route attention through queries and keys without changing the value-side evidence being retrieved. In a one-layer MDM, we prove that forward masked training strengthens evidence that is reusable in reverse queries, induces correlated forward--reverse attention routes, and yields a positively aligned shared-storage gradient component that decreases the reverse loss to first order. Controlled one-layer experiments and large-scale LLaDA/Dream experiments verify these signatures and show that they translate into improved reverse prediction.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Hierarchical Masked Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29255v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Masked diffusion language models have emerged as an alternative to autoregressive models, enabling parallel token generation. However, standard masked diffusion language models use a shared noise schedule across positions without explicitly encoding which positions should be resolved first. Many tasks exhibit a natural priority among positions: document titles and section headings can guide the generation of body text, while Sudoku cells determined directly from the initial clues can help resolve more difficult cells. To incorporate this notion of priority (or hierarchy) among token positions, we introduce the Hierarchical Masked Diffusion Language Model (HMDLM), which augments each token position with a hierarchy level available as part of the training data, and requires zero or minimal additional annotations. It factors generation into two passes: the first predicts a hierarchy distribution over masked positions, the second predicts token distributions conditioned on it. Giving each hierarchy level its own noise schedule induces a soft, controllable preference for revealing higher-priority positions earlier while keeping decoding fully parallel. Our formulation is mathematically grounded, and we re-derive the forward and reverse processes and the NELBO for this factorization. Our evaluation shows that for the task of language generation on LM1B, HMDLM reduces perplexity by more than 1.5 points over Anchored Diffusion Language Model (ADLM), a recently proposed strong diffusion baseline; on WikiHow, we see a drop in perplexity of up to 4.4 pts on ADLM and up to 7 pts against MDLM. An LLM judge on WikiHow prefers HMDLM generations (the two variants combined) more than $2\times$ as often as the baseline. On the task of solving Sudoku puzzles requiring multi-step reasoning, it improves solution accuracy by up to $8.6\%$ absolute points over existing diffusion baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[Adaptive Steering and Remasking for Safe Generation in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13043v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion Language Models(DLMs) provide a promising alternative to autoregressive language models through iterative denoising and bidirectional generation. However, their iterative generation process introduces distinct safety vulnerabilities because harmful content can emerge at arbitrary positions and persist across subsequent denoising steps. Existing defenses rely on fixed interventions or aggressive remasking, which limits adaptive control over denoising trajectories and can degrade generation quality. We propose an inference-time defense framework that combines adaptive safety steering with safety-aware remasking. Our method uses a gating direction to continuously adjust steering strength from the current denoising state and applies a steering direction to masked positions to guide subsequent predictions toward safer trajectories. Our method further employs a lightweight response detector after the first generation block to identify unsafe trajectories at an early stage. The detector triggers targeted remasking over generated content and part of the conditioning prompt, and the model regenerates the selected positions under adaptive safety steering. This design combines continuous trajectory control with explicit correction of unsafe content while requiring no modification of model parameters. Experiments on LLaDA and Dream demonstrate that our method improves robustness against diverse jailbreak attacks while preserving benign generation quality and general model capability. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DLM_Steering-C32B/.</p></details> | 23 pages, 5 figures |
| **[GRAFT: Restoring the Missing Sampling Interface in Continuous Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29136v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Continuous diffusion language models generate a whole sequence in parallel, never drawing a single word at any intermediate step; only the final step turns embeddings into text. Models such as ELF now report autoregressive-level quality at a fraction of the sampling steps of discrete-token diffusion models, and this design principle -- no step-wise discretization -- is what their own ablations credit for the gain. We show that this same principle carries a hidden cost: a collapse of the lexical long tail. Measured against held-out human text, ELF's output matches the human rate on common words almost exactly, yet its rarest words appear at only 0.30x the human rate, a gap that does not close from 105M to 652M parameters and that no sampler setting these models expose can reach. A same-size GPT-2, in contrast, spans zero to above the human rate by its decoding rule alone. We trace the difference to what continuous diffusion models lack: with no step that ever draws a discrete word, every step is instead driven by an average over the candidates still in play, and averaging a rare candidate together with the common word competing for its slot lands on the common word. An autoregressive model avoids this because its decoding rule samples one candidate rather than averaging over all of them. We propose GRAFT, a training-free method that grafts this missing choice back onto a frozen model: while the model is still undecided, GRAFT commits the rare words the average would otherwise discard, then regenerates the sequence from scratch with those commitments held in place. Across three ELF scales and on LangFlow, an independent continuous dLLM, GRAFT restores the rare-word share of generated text from 0.42-0.77x the human rate to 0.82-1.10x, a relative gain of 34-109%, and moves the entire word-frequency profile closer to human on every model tested, by 2.7x on ELF-B and 12x on LangFlow.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Model Weights: Sampling-Dependent Fairness in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27584v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models (DLMs) are emerging as a practical alternative to autoregressive language models (ARLMs), yet their fairness remains largely uncharacterized. DLMs introduce sampling controls with no direct autoregressive counterpart, such as block length and remasking strategy, that directly set their speed--quality operating point. This raises a basic question: does a DLM's measured social bias remain stable across these settings? Across LLaDA, LLaDA-1.5, and Dream on Open-BBQ, we find that all three models exhibit social bias, but both the magnitude of measured bias and the relative ordering of models by bias are highly sensitive to the sampling configuration. This sampling dependence also changes what successful debiasing means. While mitigation strategies established for ARLMs transfer effectively to DLMs and reduce bias without degrading downstream performance, sampling dependence persists even after mitigation. Our results show that fairness in DLMs cannot be characterized independently of the sampling configuration, even after debiasing. Project page is available at https://pakapaka333.github.io/Beyond-Model-Weights/.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 11 figures, 17 tables. Project page: https://pakapaka333.github.io/Beyond-Model-Weights/ ; v2: corrected an author's name in metadata (no change to content)</p></details> |
| **[DiffResearch: Accelerating Deep Research with Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28909v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep research systems iteratively plan, gather evidence, synthesize, and revise, making end-to-end latency a key bottleneck for interactive use. Almost all production systems are built on autoregressive (AR) language models and inherit left-to-right decoding in planning and long-form writing. We present DiffResearch, an open multi-agent deep research framework that places a diffusion language model (dLLM) at the core of every stage, and measure how a diffusion backbone changes latency and report quality. Holding the agentic scaffold, retrieval code, prompts, and evaluation fixed, we swap the backbone between an open diffusion model and a comparable AR model. Across 100 Deep Research Bench tasks, the diffusion backbone is roughly 6 times faster end-to-end, driven by order-of-magnitude reductions in planning and writing. We further introduce dFast, a decomposition mode that reads intermediate denoising states and extracts subqueries before generation finishes, providing a 1.99 times median speedup for decomposition in isolation with no loss of report quality. On the benchmark's RACE rubric, dFast obtains the best overall score among the three configurations. Finally, in a public system-level leaderboard snapshot, an instance built on a stronger diffusion model ranks above several commercial systems. We release DiffResearch under an Apache-2.0 license.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026 Industry Track</p></details> |
| **[Hidden Messages Between Diffusion Language Model Agents: Causal Evidence for Latent Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28905v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models form answers through a sequence of revisable continuous states, but multi-agent systems usually discard those states and make agents communicate only after decoding text. We ask whether one independent diffusion trajectory can pass a compact hidden message into another and, crucially, whether the receiver uses what that message means. In Hidden Messages, a sender compresses its denoising state into four signed dense vectors; the receiver consumes them while retaining its own context, canvas, and trajectory. Removing a message tests whether the channel matters; replacing it with a message from another example tests whether its content matters. Across controlled tasks, derangement removes a +63.6-point advantage on Tiny-A2D and a +100.0-point advantage on Dream-7B, while targeted substitutions steer the receiver toward the substituted fact. On QASC with the two supporting facts split across agents, matched communication reaches 80.8%, but a wrong-example message lowers accuracy to 46.8%, a content effect of +34.0 points. The surprising result is temporal: one late transfer reaches 87.8%, exceeding four recurrent exchanges by +7.0 points. These experiments provide causal evidence for continuous hidden communication between diffusion-LM agents and show that when information is transferred can matter more than how many times agents exchange it.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the DiffuLM Workshop at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Beyond the Sequentiality Bottleneck: A Hybrid Diffusion-Autoregressive Framework for Efficient Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28584v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autoregressive models (ARMs) face a "Sequentiality Bottleneck" in reasoning, while diffusion language models (DLMs) offer parallelism but lack semantic coherence. To addresses both limitations, we propose Collaborative Reasoning Asymmetric Learning (CoRAL), a hybrid framework via an Asymmetric Planner-Executor (APE) architecture: a DLM Planner generates K diverse reasoning trajectories in parallel, while a large ARM Executor selects the optimal plan and synthesizes the final answer. To train the Planner effectively, we introduce Reward-Guided Denoising for CoT (RDC), which integrates outcome-based rewards into the diffusion process to steer it toward goal-directed reasoning. Experiments on vision-language benchmarks show that CoRAL significantly outperforms prior diffusion-based methods and achieves competitive performance against strong ARMs, achieving both superior efficiency and quality.</p></details> |  |
| **[Exploring More, Reasoning Better: Stepwise Risk-Sensitive GRPO for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00661v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) generate text by denoising a sequence or successive blocks, allowing several tokens to be revealed in parallel. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) reuses terminal feedback across these decisions, even as their conditioning context changes. We propose stepwise risk-sensitive GRPO (StepRS-GRPO), which varies the risk coefficient of the group-advantage transformation across denoising states while retaining the underlying trainer. For binary rewards, we show that this transformation is exactly a prompt- and state-dependent rescaling of centered outcome advantages. A capability-based calibration suggests a coefficient scale, while endpoint and interpolation ablations guide schedule selection. Across multiple dLLM backbones and mathematical reasoning benchmarks, StepRS-GRPO improves both pass@1 accuracy and pass@k coverage over centered GRPO, while increasing answer diversity. In our ablation studies, mass-matched controls support the contributions of state allocation and schedule direction, and the gains persist after matching the root mean square (RMS) of the advantages to that of centered GRPO. Reasoning-trace diagnostics further show that the diversity gains from StepRS-GRPO extend beyond final-answer strings.</p></details> | <details><summary>47 pa...</summary><p>47 pages, 12 figures, 4 tables. Writing and presentation have been polished in this revision. The first two authors contributed equally</p></details> |
| **[Self-Decomposing Diffusion for Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27600v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models (DLMs) are emerging as a promising paradigm for code generation, offering the potential for fast, parallel decoding. However, generating complex programs remains challenging, as their interdependent components must maintain consistent interfaces, data flows, and shared assumptions. Aggressive parallel decoding can compromise these dependencies and undermine program correctness, while conservative decoding sacrifices efficiency. To address this trade-off, we propose planning program decomposition before implementation to coordinate interdependent units during parallel generation. We introduce Self-Decomposing Diffusion for Code (SDDC), a novel framework that decomposes complex programs and implements their units in parallel, entirely through diffusion. SDDC first generates a contract specifying the program's decomposition and inter-unit dependencies, then uses it to guide coordinated parallel implementation. Across five benchmarks and two 7B diffusion backbones, SDDC substantially improves the accuracy--latency trade-off, raising pass@1 by up to 31.1 percentage points while decoding 7.5--13.7$\times$ faster than the base models, and outperforming a fine-tuning baseline by up to 23.5 points with lower latency.</p></details> |  |
| **[Factual Access States in Diffusion Language Models: From Mechanism to Knowledge Editing and Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27580v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Factual recall is a lookup: some representation inside the network serves as the key, and what that key is made of decides which queries can retrieve the fact, what an edit must cover, and what a retriever can read. The state therefore varies with the relation's surface form and with the query direction, so the queries about one fact occupy a region of state space rather than a point, and an operation on the fact succeeds only for queries that enter it. Code: https://github.com/Quentin-Fruytier/PIG-DLM-release</p></details> |
| **[RL Post-Training Diffusion Language Models Along Their Own Generation Paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17266v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models are a promising and more efficient alternative to autoregressive models. ## Masked Diffusion
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[What Makes a Good Unmasking Policy? A Controlled Study of Learned Unmasking in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31576v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Masked diffusion language models generate tokens in parallel, but their efficiency depends on which predictions are committed. We find that labeling predictions matching the final output is overly permissive at low NFE, and hidden states are weak alone but can complement confidence. About a 10% NFE reduction suffices to offset policy overhead for policies up to 7.5M parameters across tested settings. We therefore train a policy on trajectory-contraction targets, which accept only commitments staying on a reference trajectory, using confidence and hidden states. On LLaDA-8B-Instruct, the resulting 856K-parameter policy achieves the best accuracy--NFE frontier and highest throughput on GSM8K, is competitive at moderate targets on MATH-500 and HumanEval, and largely transfers to Dream-7B-Instruct.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint.32 pages, 11 figures</p></details> |

Cola2 dynamically segments text into semantic spans, controls the compression rate through auxiliary-loss-free negative feedback, and combines reconstruction and generation supervision to learn compact, predictive latent representations. A block-causal masked diffusion decoder is trained with clean, noisy, and dropped latent conditions, while classifier-free guidance adjusts the balance between semantic reconstruction and autonomous generation. Experiments on five downstream tasks with about 210 billion training tokens validate the core designs and show competitive, unsaturated scaling. With 346.03 billion source tokens, an unconverged checkpoint reaches a 60.10 average across eight tasks and surpasses Llama-3-8B on HellaSwag and StoryCloze despite its reported pretraining budget being over 43 times larger. | **[Factual Access States in Diffusion Language Models: From Mechanism to Knowledge Editing and Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27580v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Factual recall is a lookup: some representation inside the network serves as the key, and what that key is made of decides which queries can retrieve the fact, what an edit must cover, and what a retriever can read. ## Constrained Sampling

| **[ShIOEnv: A Command Evaluation Environment for Grammar-Constrained Synthesis and Execution Behavior Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18374v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce ShIOEnv, a Gymnasium-compatible Bash environment for synthesizing execution-annotated shell input traces. Existing datasets, such as NL2CMD and other Bash command corpora, do not include complete execution artifacts (stdout, stderr, exit status, and changes to the system's state) associated with a command's execution. We prepare 1.6M samples using grammar-constrained and unconstrained synthesis and collect their execution artifacts for 86 Linux/Bash utilities. Using this dataset, we train small language models (CodeT5) and evaluate their accuracy in predicting the actual execution behavior of attacker-sourced Bash commands. To mitigate representation inconsistency, we design a cross-subgraph consistency alignment mechanism.
